Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: My doctors keep telling me I’m too thin and need to eat more, but I honestly don’t see what all the fuss is about. I feel like I’m just being careful with my health and doing what's best for my heart.\n\nDoctor: Do you strongly fear gaining weight or feel very upset about the idea of being overweight, even if other people say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even think about eating rich foods like butter, I get a tight feeling in my chest and picture my arteries clogging up right away. When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all—I just see soft spots on my belly that I still need to trim down. To me, staying lean is just a matter of health and self-discipline, not something to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating mainly because you are afraid of gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it more because you worry that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it’s really a mix of both. I do worry about my belly looking soft or popping out if I let myself go, but my main goal is protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear so I don't end up like my friends. To me, keeping a strict limit on these foods is just the only disciplined way to stay healthy.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict food, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mostly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will damage your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a mix of both for me. I worry that \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream will clog my arteries and damage my heart, but I also have a real fear of gaining weight and seeing my belly look soft or pop out. Keeping that weight off and maintaining this discipline is the only way I feel I can stay healthy and avoid heart disease.\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating less, which feels stronger: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: For me, those two fears are completely tied together. Gaining weight and seeing my belly pop out feels like a direct threat to my heart, making me picture my arteries clogging up. When I think about foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I associate them with both weight gain and heart trouble, so staying lean feels like the only sensible way to protect my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you feel you are actually too thin or underweight, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when others say you are very thin?"

I feel like I’m just being careful with my health and doing what's best for my heart.\n\nDoctor: Do you strongly fear gaining weight or feel very upset about the idea of being overweight, even if other people say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even think about eating rich foods like butter, I get a tight feeling in my chest and picture my arteries clogging up right away. When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all—I just see soft spots on my belly that I still need to trim down. To me, staying lean is just a matter of health and self-discipline, not something to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating mainly because you are afraid of gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it more because you worry that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it’s really a mix of both. I do worry about my belly looking soft or popping out if I let myself go, but my main goal is protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear so I don't end up like my friends. To me, keeping a strict limit on these foods is just the only disciplined way to stay healthy.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict food, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mostly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will damage your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a mix of both for me. I worry that \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream will clog my arteries and damage my heart, but I also have a real fear of gaining weight and seeing my belly look soft or pop out. Keeping that weight off and maintaining this discipline is the only way I feel I can stay healthy and avoid heart disease.\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating less, which feels stronger: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: For me, those two fears are completely tied together. Gaining weight and seeing my belly pop out feels like a direct threat to my heart, making me picture my arteries clogging up. When I think about foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I associate them with both weight gain and heart trouble, so staying lean feels like the only sensible way to protect my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you feel you are actually too thin or underweight, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when others say you are very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all, despite what the doctors say. I mostly just notice spots where my belly still looks soft and think I could stand to trim that down a bit more to keep my heart in good shape. To me, my weight isn't a danger—it's just a sign of good discipline and staying healthy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think you are too thin or underweight, or do you feel you still need to lose weight even when other people say you are already very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I really don't see \"too thin\"—I just see areas like my belly that still look soft and could stand to be trimmed down a bit more to protect my heart. While others worry, staying lean just feels like sensible discipline at my age, and I'm actually quietly pleased when I see the number on the scale go down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally restricting how much you eat, fasting, or exercising a lot to lose weight or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do strictly limit what I eat. Most days I just make a small bowl of oatmeal for my wife and me to share in the morning, and then I'll just pick at some vegetables or half an apple later. I avoid rich foods like butter or ice cream to prevent weight gain and protect my heart, which just feels like sensible discipline for an older man who isn't very active anymore."
  }

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: My doctors keep telling me I’m too thin and need to eat more, but I honestly don’t see what all the fuss is about. I feel like I’m just being careful with my health and doing what's best for my heart.\n\nDoctor: Do you strongly fear gaining weight or feel very upset about the idea of being overweight, even if other people say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even think about eating rich foods like butter, I get a tight feeling in my chest and picture my arteries clogging up right away. When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all—I just see soft spots on my belly that I still need to trim down. To me, staying lean is just a matter of health and self-discipline, not something to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating mainly because you are afraid of gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it more because you worry that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it’s really a mix of both. I do worry about my belly looking soft or popping out if I let myself go, but my main goal is protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear so I don't end up like my friends. To me, keeping a strict limit on these foods is just the only disciplined way to stay healthy.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict food, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mostly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will damage your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a mix of both for me. I worry that \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream will clog my arteries and damage my heart, but I also have a real fear of gaining weight and seeing my belly look soft or pop out. Keeping that weight off and maintaining this discipline is the only way I feel I can stay healthy and avoid heart disease.\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating less, which feels stronger: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: For me, those two fears are completely tied together. Gaining weight and seeing my belly pop out feels like a direct threat to my heart, making me picture my arteries clogging up. When I think about foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I associate them with both weight gain and heart trouble, so staying lean feels like the only sensible way to protect my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you feel you are actually too thin or underweight, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when others say you are very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all, despite what the doctors say. I mostly just notice spots where my belly still looks soft and think I could stand to trim that down a bit more to keep my heart in good shape. To me, my weight isn't a danger—it's just a sign of good discipline and staying healthy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think you are too thin or underweight, or do you feel you still need to lose weight even when other people say you are already very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I really don't see \"too thin\"—I just see areas like my belly that still look soft and could stand to be trimmed down a bit more to protect my heart. While others worry, staying lean just feels like sensible discipline at my age, and I'm actually quietly pleased when I see the number on the scale go down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally restricting how much you eat, fasting, or exercising a lot to lose weight or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do strictly limit what I eat. Most days I just make a small bowl of oatmeal for my wife and me to share in the morning, and then I'll just pick at some vegetables or half an apple later. I avoid rich foods like butter or ice cream to prevent weight gain and protect my heart, which just feels like sensible discipline for an older man who isn't very active anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your body weight or shape is too low or that you need to gain weight, or do you mainly feel you should keep losing or maintaining weight to stay healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't feel my weight is too low at all, and I certainly don't think I need to gain weight. When I look in the mirror, I just see soft areas on my belly that I could still trim down to protect my heart. Staying lean and keeping the scale down feels like healthy discipline, especially compared to the heart issues my buddies are facing.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating and weight, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mainly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will harm your health or heart?"
  }

However, I also get very anxious about gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft, and imagining eating "unsafe" foods like butter or ice cream makes me picture both clogged arteries and my belly popping out.

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: My doctors keep telling me I’m too thin and need to eat more, but I honestly don’t see what all the fuss is about. I feel like I’m just being careful with my health and doing what's best for my heart.\n\nDoctor: Do you strongly fear gaining weight or feel very upset about the idea of being overweight, even if other people say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even think about eating rich foods like butter, I get a tight feeling in my chest and picture my arteries clogging up right away. To me, staying lean is just a matter of health and self-discipline, not something to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating mainly because you are afraid of gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it more because you worry that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it’s really a mix of both. I do worry about my belly looking soft or popping out if I let myself go, but my main goal is protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear so I don't end up like my friends. To me, keeping a strict limit on these foods is just the only disciplined way to stay healthy.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict food, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mostly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will damage your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a mix of both for me. I worry that \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream will clog my arteries and damage my heart, but I also have a real fear of gaining weight and seeing my belly look soft or pop out. Keeping that weight off and maintaining this discipline is the only way I feel I can stay healthy and avoid heart disease.\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating less, which feels stronger: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: For me, those two fears are completely tied together. Gaining weight and seeing my belly pop out feels like a direct threat to my heart, making me picture my arteries clogging up. When I think about foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I associate them with both weight gain and heart trouble, so staying lean feels like the only sensible way to protect my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you feel you are actually too thin or underweight, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when others say you are very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all, despite what the doctors say. I mostly just notice spots where my belly still looks soft and think I could stand to trim that down a bit more to keep my heart in good shape. To me, my weight isn't a danger—it's just a sign of good discipline and staying healthy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think you are too thin or underweight, or do you feel you still need to lose weight even when other people say you are already very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I really don't see \"too thin\"—I just see areas like my belly that still look soft and could stand to be trimmed down a bit more to protect my heart. While others worry, staying lean just feels like sensible discipline at my age, and I'm actually quietly pleased when I see the number on the scale go down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally restricting how much you eat, fasting, or exercising a lot to lose weight or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do strictly limit what I eat. Most days I just make a small bowl of oatmeal for my wife and me to share in the morning, and then I'll just pick at some vegetables or half an apple later. I avoid rich foods like butter or ice cream to prevent weight gain and protect my heart, which just feels like sensible discipline for an older man who isn't very active anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your body weight or shape is too low or that you need to gain weight, or do you mainly feel you should keep losing or maintaining weight to stay healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't feel my weight is too low at all, and I certainly don't think I need to gain weight. When I look in the mirror, I just see soft areas on my belly that I could still trim down to protect my heart. Staying lean and keeping the scale down feels like healthy discipline, especially compared to the heart issues my buddies are facing.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating and weight, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mainly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a combination of both, as I am deeply concerned about protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear, especially after seeing my friends struggle with heart disease. However, I also get very anxious about gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft, and imagining eating \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream makes me picture both clogged arteries and my belly popping out.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating, is your main fear gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it mostly that certain foods will harm your health or heart?"

His naturally meticulous, perfectionistic streak—once applied to woodworking and art tours—shifted toward tightly controlling his meals, especially as he watched several close friends from his senior walking club struggle with heart disease and frailty. When his wife had a minor stroke last year, he took on more caregiving responsibilities and quietly doubled down on simplifying meals to \"healthy essentials,\" proud that he could \"keep them both on track\" even as neighbors and his walking group commented on how thin he had become. The situation came to clinical attention after a routine cardiology follow-up showed further weight loss and concerning lab abnormalities; his cardiologist noted his insistence that the weight was \"only good discipline\" and referred him for psychiatric evaluation when he continued to restrict food despite medical advice to regain weight.\n- Chief Complaint: My doctors keep telling me I’m too thin and need to eat more, but I honestly don’t see what the fuss is about—I feel like I’m just being careful with my health.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S043 (Restrictive_Intake): Most days I just make us a small bowl of oatmeal to share in the morning and then pick at some vegetables or half an apple later, telling myself an old man who isn’t out building houses anymore doesn’t need more than that, even if my stomach growls a bit at night. \n- S044 (Low_Weight): I know my pants hang off me now and my belt’s on the last notch, but I keep thinking that at my age it’s better to be on the lean side, so I’ve been quietly pleased to see the number on the scale inch down instead of up. \n- S045 (Fear_of_Weight_Gain): If I even imagine putting butter on my toast or having a scoop of ice cream with the grandkids, I get this tight feeling in my chest and picture my arteries clogging up and my belly popping out, so I just stick to my “safe” foods. \n- S046 (Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance): When I look in the mirror I don’t see what the doctors call ‘too thin’—I mostly see the spots where my belly still looks soft and I think, ‘you could stand to trim that down a bit more if you want to keep your heart in good shape.’ \n- S084 (Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition): Everyone keeps acting like I’m in danger because of my weight, but to me it just feels like I’ve finally got some control and discipline, and compared to my buddies dropping from heart attacks, eating light and staying skinny seems like the sensible, healthy thing to do.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve started skipping the coffee meetups after my walking group because I don’t want people commenting on what I order, and some days I feel too drained from eating so little to keep up with my wife’s appointments and house chores the way I used to.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: My doctors keep telling me I’m too thin and need to eat more, but I honestly don’t see what the fuss is about—I feel like I’m just being careful with my health.\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: S045, S046\nAnswer strategy: Explain that for you, these two fears are completely intertwined. Express that gaining weight or seeing your belly look soft is directly connected in your mind to your arteries clogging up and damaging your heart. Mention that if you imagine eating 'unsafe' foods like butter or ice cream, you immediately picture both your belly popping out and your heart health failing, as staying lean is how you believe you keep your heart safe."

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: My doctors keep telling me I’m too thin and need to eat more, but I honestly don’t see what all the fuss is about. I feel like I’m just being careful with my health and doing what's best for my heart.\n\nDoctor: Do you strongly fear gaining weight or feel very upset about the idea of being overweight, even if other people say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even think about eating rich foods like butter, I get a tight feeling in my chest and picture my arteries clogging up right away. When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all—I just see soft spots on my belly that I still need to trim down. To me, staying lean is just a matter of health and self-discipline, not something to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating mainly because you are afraid of gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it more because you worry that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it’s really a mix of both. I do worry about my belly looking soft or popping out if I let myself go, but my main goal is protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear so I don't end up like my friends. To me, keeping a strict limit on these foods is just the only disciplined way to stay healthy.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict food, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mostly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will damage your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a mix of both for me. I worry that \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream will clog my arteries and damage my heart, but I also have a real fear of gaining weight and seeing my belly look soft or pop out. Keeping that weight off and maintaining this discipline is the only way I feel I can stay healthy and avoid heart disease.\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating less, which feels stronger: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: For me, those two fears are completely tied together. Gaining weight and seeing my belly pop out feels like a direct threat to my heart, making me picture my arteries clogging up. When I think about foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I associate them with both weight gain and heart trouble, so staying lean feels like the only sensible way to protect my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you feel you are actually too thin or underweight, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when others say you are very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all, despite what the doctors say. I mostly just notice spots where my belly still looks soft and think I could stand to trim that down a bit more to keep my heart in good shape. To me, my weight isn't a danger—it's just a sign of good discipline and staying healthy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think you are too thin or underweight, or do you feel you still need to lose weight even when other people say you are already very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I really don't see \"too thin\"—I just see areas like my belly that still look soft and could stand to be trimmed down a bit more to protect my heart. While others worry, staying lean just feels like sensible discipline at my age, and I'm actually quietly pleased when I see the number on the scale go down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally restricting how much you eat, fasting, or exercising a lot to lose weight or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do strictly limit what I eat. Most days I just make a small bowl of oatmeal for my wife and me to share in the morning, and then I'll just pick at some vegetables or half an apple later. I avoid rich foods like butter or ice cream to prevent weight gain and protect my heart, which just feels like sensible discipline for an older man who isn't very active anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your body weight or shape is too low or that you need to gain weight, or do you mainly feel you should keep losing or maintaining weight to stay healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't feel my weight is too low at all, and I certainly don't think I need to gain weight. When I look in the mirror, I just see soft areas on my belly that I could still trim down to protect my heart. Staying lean and keeping the scale down feels like healthy discipline, especially compared to the heart issues my buddies are facing.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating and weight, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mainly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a combination of both, as I am deeply concerned about protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear, especially after seeing my friends struggle with heart disease. However, I also get very anxious about gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft, and imagining eating \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream makes me picture both clogged arteries and my belly popping out.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating, is your main fear gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it mostly that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both fears for me. When I think about eating foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I picture my arteries clogging up and harming my heart. At the same time, I worry about my belly popping out or looking soft, which makes me feel like I need to keep trimming down.\n\nDoctor: If you had to choose, which is stronger for you: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that eating certain foods will damage your health or heart?"
  }

I really can't separate the two because they are completely intertwined in my mind. Gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft is directly connected to my arteries clogging up and damaging my heart. If I even imagine eating butter or ice cream, I picture my belly popping out and my heart health failing all at once, because staying lean is how I keep my heart safe.

His naturally meticulous, perfectionistic streak—once applied to woodworking and art tours—shifted toward tightly controlling his meals, especially as he watched several close friends from his senior walking club struggle with heart disease and frailty. When his wife had a minor stroke last year, he took on more caregiving responsibilities and quietly doubled down on simplifying meals to \"healthy essentials,\" proud that he could \"keep them both on track\" even as neighbors and his walking group commented on how thin he had become. The situation came to clinical attention after a routine cardiology follow-up showed further weight loss and concerning lab abnormalities; his cardiologist noted his insistence that the weight was \"only good discipline\" and referred him for psychiatric evaluation when he continued to restrict food despite medical advice to regain weight.\n- Chief Complaint: My doctors keep telling me I’m too thin and need to eat more, but I honestly don’t see what the fuss is about—I feel like I’m just being careful with my health.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S043 (Restrictive_Intake): Most days I just make us a small bowl of oatmeal to share in the morning and then pick at some vegetables or half an apple later, telling myself an old man who isn’t out building houses anymore doesn’t need more than that, even if my stomach growls a bit at night. \n- S044 (Low_Weight): I know my pants hang off me now and my belt’s on the last notch, but I keep thinking that at my age it’s better to be on the lean side, so I’ve been quietly pleased to see the number on the scale inch down instead of up. \n- S045 (Fear_of_Weight_Gain): If I even imagine putting butter on my toast or having a scoop of ice cream with the grandkids, I get this tight feeling in my chest and picture my arteries clogging up and my belly popping out, so I just stick to my “safe” foods. \n- S046 (Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance): When I look in the mirror I don’t see what the doctors call ‘too thin’—I mostly see the spots where my belly still looks soft and I think, ‘you could stand to trim that down a bit more if you want to keep your heart in good shape.’ \n- S084 (Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition): Everyone keeps acting like I’m in danger because of my weight, but to me it just feels like I’ve finally got some control and discipline, and compared to my buddies dropping from heart attacks, eating light and staying skinny seems like the sensible, healthy thing to do.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve started skipping the coffee meetups after my walking group because I don’t want people commenting on what I order, and some days I feel too drained from eating so little to keep up with my wife’s appointments and house chores the way I used to.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: My doctors keep telling me I’m too thin and need to eat more, but I honestly don’t see what the fuss is about—I feel like I’m just being careful with my health.\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: Chief Complaint, S044 (Low_Weight), S046 (Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance), S084 (Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition)\nAnswer strategy: Explain that you do not see yourself as 'too thin' or underweight; rather, you see your weight as a sign of healthy discipline. Mention that when you look in the mirror, you don't see what the doctors see—instead, you notice areas around your belly that still look soft and could be trimmed down further to protect your heart. Confess that you feel quietly pleased when the number on the scale goes down, as staying lean feels like the safest, most sensible path at your age."

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: My doctors keep telling me I’m too thin and need to eat more, but I honestly don’t see what all the fuss is about. I feel like I’m just being careful with my health and doing what's best for my heart.\n\nDoctor: Do you strongly fear gaining weight or feel very upset about the idea of being overweight, even if other people say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even think about eating rich foods like butter, I get a tight feeling in my chest and picture my arteries clogging up right away. When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all—I just see soft spots on my belly that I still need to trim down. To me, staying lean is just a matter of health and self-discipline, not something to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating mainly because you are afraid of gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it more because you worry that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it’s really a mix of both. I do worry about my belly looking soft or popping out if I let myself go, but my main goal is protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear so I don't end up like my friends. To me, keeping a strict limit on these foods is just the only disciplined way to stay healthy.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict food, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mostly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will damage your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a mix of both for me. I worry that \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream will clog my arteries and damage my heart, but I also have a real fear of gaining weight and seeing my belly look soft or pop out. Keeping that weight off and maintaining this discipline is the only way I feel I can stay healthy and avoid heart disease.\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating less, which feels stronger: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: For me, those two fears are completely tied together. Gaining weight and seeing my belly pop out feels like a direct threat to my heart, making me picture my arteries clogging up. When I think about foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I associate them with both weight gain and heart trouble, so staying lean feels like the only sensible way to protect my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you feel you are actually too thin or underweight, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when others say you are very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all, despite what the doctors say. I mostly just notice spots where my belly still looks soft and think I could stand to trim that down a bit more to keep my heart in good shape. To me, my weight isn't a danger—it's just a sign of good discipline and staying healthy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think you are too thin or underweight, or do you feel you still need to lose weight even when other people say you are already very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I really don't see \"too thin\"—I just see areas like my belly that still look soft and could stand to be trimmed down a bit more to protect my heart. While others worry, staying lean just feels like sensible discipline at my age, and I'm actually quietly pleased when I see the number on the scale go down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally restricting how much you eat, fasting, or exercising a lot to lose weight or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do strictly limit what I eat. Most days I just make a small bowl of oatmeal for my wife and me to share in the morning, and then I'll just pick at some vegetables or half an apple later. I avoid rich foods like butter or ice cream to prevent weight gain and protect my heart, which just feels like sensible discipline for an older man who isn't very active anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your body weight or shape is too low or that you need to gain weight, or do you mainly feel you should keep losing or maintaining weight to stay healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't feel my weight is too low at all, and I certainly don't think I need to gain weight. When I look in the mirror, I just see soft areas on my belly that I could still trim down to protect my heart. Staying lean and keeping the scale down feels like healthy discipline, especially compared to the heart issues my buddies are facing.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating and weight, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mainly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a combination of both, as I am deeply concerned about protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear, especially after seeing my friends struggle with heart disease. However, I also get very anxious about gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft, and imagining eating \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream makes me picture both clogged arteries and my belly popping out.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating, is your main fear gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it mostly that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both fears for me. When I think about eating foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I picture my arteries clogging up and harming my heart. At the same time, I worry about my belly popping out or looking soft, which makes me feel like I need to keep trimming down.\n\nDoctor: If you had to choose, which is stronger for you: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that eating certain foods will damage your health or heart?\n\nPatient: I really can't separate the two because they are completely intertwined in my mind. Gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft is directly connected to my arteries clogging up and damaging my heart. If I even imagine eating butter or ice cream, I picture my belly popping out and my heart health failing all at once, because staying lean is how I keep my heart safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you are already underweight or too thin, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when other people tell you you are very thin?"

I don't see myself as underweight or too thin at all; when I look in the mirror, I just notice areas on my belly that still look soft and could be trimmed down to protect my heart. Staying lean feels like the safest, most sensible path for me at my age, so I'm actually quietly pleased when the number on the scale goes down. To me, it's just a sign of healthy discipline, not a medical problem.

When his wife had a minor stroke last year, he took on more caregiving responsibilities and quietly doubled down on simplifying meals to \"healthy essentials,\" proud that he could \"keep them both on track\" even as neighbors and his walking group commented on how thin he had become. The situation came to clinical attention after a routine cardiology follow-up showed further weight loss and concerning lab abnormalities; his cardiologist noted his insistence that the weight was \"only good discipline\" and referred him for psychiatric evaluation when he continued to restrict food despite medical advice to regain weight.\n- Chief Complaint: My doctors keep telling me I’m too thin and need to eat more, but I honestly don’t see what the fuss is about—I feel like I’m just being careful with my health.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S043 (Restrictive_Intake): Most days I just make us a small bowl of oatmeal to share in the morning and then pick at some vegetables or half an apple later, telling myself an old man who isn’t out building houses anymore better to be on the lean side, so I’ve been quietly pleased to see the number on the scale inch down instead of up. \n- S045 (Fear_of_Weight_Gain): If I even imagine putting butter on my toast or having a scoop of ice cream with the grandkids, I get this tight feeling in my chest and picture my arteries clogging up and my belly popping out, so I just stick to my “safe” foods. \n- S046 (Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance): When I look in the mirror I don’t see what the doctors call ‘too thin’—I mostly see the spots where my belly still looks soft and I think, ‘you could stand to trim that down a bit more if you want to keep your heart in good shape.’ \n- S084 (Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition): Everyone keeps acting like I’m in danger because of my weight, but to me it just feels like I’ve finally got some control and discipline, and compared to my buddies dropping from heart attacks, eating light and staying skinny seems like the sensible, healthy thing to do.\n▸     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: My doctors keep telling me I’m too thin and need to eat more, but I honestly don’t see what all the fuss is about. I feel like I’m just being careful with my health and doing what's best for my heart.\n\nDoctor: Do you strongly fear gaining weight or feel very upset about the idea of being overweight, even if other people say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even think about eating rich foods like butter, I get a tight feeling in my chest and picture my arteries clogging up right away. When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all—I just see soft spots on my belly that I still need to trim down. To me, staying lean is just a matter of health and self-discipline, not something to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating mainly because you are afraid of gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it more because you worry that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it’s really a mix of both. I do worry about my belly looking soft or popping out if I let myself go, but my main goal is protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear so I don't end up like my friends. To me, keeping a strict limit on these foods is just the only disciplined way to stay healthy.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict food, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mostly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will damage your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a mix of both for me. I worry that \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream will clog my arteries and damage my heart, but I also have a real fear of gaining weight and seeing my belly look soft or pop out. Keeping that weight off and maintaining this discipline is the only way I feel I can stay healthy and avoid heart disease.\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating less, which feels stronger: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: For me, those two fears are completely tied together. Gaining weight and seeing my belly pop out feels like a direct threat to my heart, making me picture my arteries clogging up. When I think about foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I associate them with both weight gain and heart trouble, so staying lean feels like the only sensible way to protect my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you feel you are actually too thin or underweight, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when others say you are very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all, despite what the doctors say. I mostly just notice spots where my belly still looks soft and think I could stand to trim that down a bit more to keep my heart in good shape. To me, my weight isn't a danger—it's just a sign of good discipline and staying healthy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think you are too thin or underweight, or do you feel you still need to lose weight even when other people say you are already very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I really don't see \"too thin\"—I just see areas like my belly that still look soft and could stand to be trimmed down a bit more to protect my heart. While others worry, staying lean just feels like sensible discipline at my age, and I'm actually quietly pleased when I see the number on the scale go down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally restricting how much you eat, fasting, or exercising a lot to lose weight or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do strictly limit what I eat. Most days I just make a small bowl of oatmeal for my wife and me to share in the morning, and then I'll just pick at some vegetables or half an apple later. I avoid rich foods like butter or ice cream to prevent weight gain and protect my heart, which just feels like sensible discipline for an older man who isn't very active anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your body weight or shape is too low or that you need to gain weight, or do you mainly feel you should keep losing or maintaining weight to stay healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't feel my weight is too low at all, and I certainly don't think I need to gain weight. When I look in the mirror, I just see soft areas on my belly that I could still trim down to protect my heart. Staying lean and keeping the scale down feels like healthy discipline, especially compared to the heart issues my buddies are facing.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating and weight, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mainly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a combination of both, as I am deeply concerned about protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear, especially after seeing my friends struggle with heart disease. However, I also get very anxious about gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft, and imagining eating \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream makes me picture both clogged arteries and my belly popping out.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating, is your main fear gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it mostly that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both fears for me. When I think about eating foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I picture my arteries clogging up and harming my heart. At the same time, I worry about my belly popping out or looking soft, which makes me feel like I need to keep trimming down.\n\nDoctor: If you had to choose, which is stronger for you: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that eating certain foods will damage your health or heart?\n\nPatient: I really can't separate the two because they are completely intertwined in my mind. Gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft is directly connected to my arteries clogging up and damaging my heart. If I even imagine eating butter or ice cream, I picture my belly popping out and my heart health failing all at once, because staying lean is how I keep my heart safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you are already underweight or too thin, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when other people tell you you are very thin?\n\nPatient: I don't see myself as underweight or too thin at all; when I look in the mirror, I just notice areas on my belly that still look soft and could be trimmed down to protect my heart. Staying lean feels like the safest, most sensible path for me at my age, so I'm actually quietly pleased when the number on the scale goes down. To me, it's just a sign of healthy discipline, not a medical problem.\n\nDoctor: If your doctor told you that your heart was completely healthy and the foods you avoid would not harm it, would you still strongly restrict food because of fear of gaining weight or becoming fat?"

When his wife had a minor stroke last year, he took on more caregiving responsibilities and quietly doubled down on simplifying meals to \"healthy essentials,\" proud that he could \"keep them both on track\" even as neighbors and his walking group commented on how thin he had become. The situation came to clinical attention after a routine cardiology follow-up showed further weight loss and concerning lab abnormalities; his cardiologist noted his insistence that the weight was     "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: My doctors keep telling me I’m too thin and need to eat more, but I honestly don’t see what all the fuss is about. I feel like I’m just being careful with my health and doing what's best for my heart.\n\nDoctor: Do you strongly fear gaining weight or feel very upset about the idea of being overweight, even if other people say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even think about eating rich foods like butter, I get a tight feeling in my chest and picture my arteries clogging up right away. When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all—I just see soft spots on my belly that I still need to trim down. To me, staying lean is just a matter of health and self-discipline, not something to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating mainly because you are afraid of gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it more because you worry that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it’s really a mix of both. I do worry about my belly looking soft or popping out if I let myself go, but my main goal is protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear so I don't end up like my friends. To me, keeping a strict limit on these foods is just the only disciplined way to stay healthy.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict food, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mostly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will damage your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a mix of both for me. I worry that \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream will clog my arteries and damage my heart, but I also have a real fear of gaining weight and seeing my belly look soft or pop out. Keeping that weight off and maintaining this discipline is the only way I feel I can stay healthy and avoid heart disease.\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating less, which feels stronger: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: For me, those two fears are completely tied together. Gaining weight and seeing my belly pop out feels like a direct threat to my heart, making me picture my arteries clogging up. When I think about foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I associate them with both weight gain and heart trouble, so staying lean feels like the only sensible way to protect my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you feel you are actually too thin or underweight, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when others say you are very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all, despite what the doctors say. I mostly just notice spots where my belly still looks soft and think I could stand to trim that down a bit more to keep my heart in good shape. To me, my weight isn't a danger—it's just a sign of good discipline and staying healthy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think you are too thin or underweight, or do you feel you still need to lose weight even when other people say you are already very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I really don't see \"too thin\"—I just see areas like my belly that still look soft and could stand to be trimmed down a bit more to protect my heart. While others worry, staying lean just feels like sensible discipline at my age, and I'm actually quietly pleased when I see the number on the scale go down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally restricting how much you eat, fasting, or exercising a lot to lose weight or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do strictly limit what I eat. Most days I just make a small bowl of oatmeal for my wife and me to share in the morning, and then I'll just pick at some vegetables or half an apple later. I avoid rich foods like butter or ice cream to prevent weight gain and protect my heart, which just feels like sensible discipline for an older man who isn't very active anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your body weight or shape is too low or that you need to gain weight, or do you mainly feel you should keep losing or maintaining weight to stay healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't feel my weight is too low at all, and I certainly don't think I need to gain weight. When I look in the mirror, I just see soft areas on my belly that I could still trim down to protect my heart. Staying lean and keeping the scale down feels like healthy discipline, especially compared to the heart issues my buddies are facing.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating and weight, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mainly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a combination of both, as I am deeply concerned about protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear, especially after seeing my friends struggle with heart disease. However, I also get very anxious about gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft, and imagining eating \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream makes me picture both clogged arteries and my belly popping out.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating, is your main fear gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it mostly that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both fears for me. When I think about eating foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I picture my arteries clogging up and harming my heart. At the same time, I worry about my belly popping out or looking soft, which makes me feel like I need to keep trimming down.\n\nDoctor: If you had to choose, which is stronger for you: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that eating certain foods will damage your health or heart?\n\nPatient: I really can't separate the two because they are completely intertwined in my mind. Gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft is directly connected to my arteries clogging up and damaging my heart. If I even imagine eating butter or ice cream, I picture my belly popping out and my heart health failing all at once, because staying lean is how I keep my heart safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you are already underweight or too thin, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when other people tell you you are very thin?\n\nPatient: I don't see myself as underweight or too thin at all; when I look in the mirror, I just notice areas on my belly that still look soft and could be trimmed down to protect my heart. Staying lean feels like the safest, most sensible path for me at my age, so I'm actually quietly pleased when the number on the scale goes down. To me, it's just a sign of healthy discipline, not a medical problem.\n\nDoctor: If your doctor told you that your heart was completely healthy and the foods you avoid would not harm it, would you still strongly restrict food because of fear of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Even if my heart were completely out of the equation, the idea of eating those richer foods still causes me intense anxiety. Just imagining it brings back that tight feeling in my chest and the fear of my belly looking soft or popping out in the mirror. That fear of gaining weight and losing control over my shape is still very real and hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: If you were told that eating richer foods would not affect your heart or health at all, would you still avoid them mainly because you fear gaining weight or becoming fat?"
  }

I dread the thought of gaining weight, seeing the scale go up, or having my belly look soft and "pop out." Just thinking about losing control of my weight brings a tight, anxious sensation to my chest because I prefer staying lean.

If I even imagine eating butter or ice cream, I picture my belly popping out and my heart health failing all at once, because staying lean is how I keep my heart safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you are already underweight or too thin, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when other people tell you you are very thin?\n\nPatient: I don't see myself as underweight or too thin at all; when I look in the mirror, I just notice areas on my belly that still look soft and could be trimmed down to protect my heart. Staying lean feels like the safest, most sensible path for me at my age, so I'm actually quietly pleased when the number on the scale goes down. To me, it's just a sign of healthy discipline, not a medical problem.\n\nDoctor: If your doctor told you that your heart was completely healthy and the foods you avoid would not harm it, would you still strongly restrict food because of fear of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Even if my heart were completely out of the equation, the idea of eating those richer foods still causes me intense anxiety. Just imagining it brings back that tight feeling in my chest and the fear of my belly looking soft or popping out in the mirror. That fear of gaining weight and losing control over my shape is still very real and hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: If you were told that eating richer foods would not affect your heart or health at all, would you still avoid them mainly because you fear gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Yes, even if heart health weren't a factor, the idea of eating richer foods still causes me deep anxiety. I dread the thought of gaining weight, seeing the scale go up, or having my belly look soft and \"pop out.\" Just thinking about losing control of my weight brings a tight, anxious sensation to my chest because I prefer staying lean."
  }
]

I do worry about my belly looking soft or popping out if I let myself go, but my main goal is protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear so I don't end up like my friends. Keeping that weight off and maintaining this discipline is the only way I feel I can stay healthy and avoid heart disease.\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating less, which feels stronger: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: For me, those two fears are completely tied together. Gaining weight and seeing my belly pop out feels like a direct threat to my heart, making me picture my arteries clogging up. I mostly just notice spots where my belly still looks soft and think I could stand to trim that down a bit more to keep my heart in good shape. To me, my weight isn't a danger—it's just a sign of good discipline and staying healthy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think you are too thin or underweight, or do you feel you still need to lose weight even when other people say you are already very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I really don't see \"too thin\"—I just see areas like my belly that still look soft and could stand to be trimmed down a bit more to protect my heart. While others worry, staying lean just feels like sensible discipline at my age, and I'm actually quietly pleased when I see the number on the scale go down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally restricting how much you eat, fasting, or exercising a lot to lose weight or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do strictly limit what I eat. Most days I just make a small bowl of oatmeal for my wife and me to share in the morning, and then I'll just pick at some vegetables or half an apple later. I avoid rich foods like butter or ice cream to prevent weight gain and protect my heart, which just feels like sensible discipline for an older man who isn't very active anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your body weight or shape is too low or that you need to gain weight, or do you mainly feel you should keep losing or maintaining weight to stay healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't feel my weight is too low at all, and I certainly don't think I need to gain weight. When I look in the mirror, I just see soft areas on my belly that I could still trim down to protect my heart. Staying lean and keeping the scale down feels like healthy discipline, especially compared to the heart issues my buddies are facing.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating and weight, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mainly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a combination of both, as I am deeply concerned about protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear, especially after seeing my friends struggle with heart disease. However, I also get very anxious about gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft, and imagining eating \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream makes me picture both clogged arteries and my belly popping out.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating, is your main fear gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it mostly that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both fears for me. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: My doctors keep telling me I’m too thin and need to eat more, but I honestly don’t see what all the fuss is about. I feel like I’m just being careful with my health and doing what's best for my heart.\n\nDoctor: Do you strongly fear gaining weight or feel very upset about the idea of being overweight, even if other people say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: Yes, if I even think about eating rich foods like butter, I get a tight feeling in my chest and picture my arteries clogging up right away. When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all—I just see soft spots on my belly that I still need to trim down. To me, staying lean is just a matter of health and self-discipline, not something to worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid eating mainly because you are afraid of gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it more because you worry that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: Honestly, it’s really a mix of both. I do worry about my belly looking soft or popping out if I let myself go, but my main goal is protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear so I don't end up like my friends. To me, keeping a strict limit on these foods is just the only disciplined way to stay healthy.\n\nDoctor: When you restrict food, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mostly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will damage your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a mix of both for me. I worry that \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream will clog my arteries and damage my heart, but I also have a real fear of gaining weight and seeing my belly look soft or pop out. Keeping that weight off and maintaining this discipline is the only way I feel I can stay healthy and avoid heart disease.\n\nDoctor: When you think about eating less, which feels stronger: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: For me, those two fears are completely tied together. Gaining weight and seeing my belly pop out feels like a direct threat to my heart, making me picture my arteries clogging up. When I think about foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I associate them with both weight gain and heart trouble, so staying lean feels like the only sensible way to protect my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you feel you are actually too thin or underweight, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when others say you are very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all, despite what the doctors say. I mostly just notice spots where my belly still looks soft and think I could stand to trim that down a bit more to keep my heart in good shape. To me, my weight isn't a danger—it's just a sign of good discipline and staying healthy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think you are too thin or underweight, or do you feel you still need to lose weight even when other people say you are already very thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I really don't see \"too thin\"—I just see areas like my belly that still look soft and could stand to be trimmed down a bit more to protect my heart. While others worry, staying lean just feels like sensible discipline at my age, and I'm actually quietly pleased when I see the number on the scale go down.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally restricting how much you eat, fasting, or exercising a lot to lose weight or prevent weight gain?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do strictly limit what I eat. Most days I just make a small bowl of oatmeal for my wife and me to share in the morning, and then I'll just pick at some vegetables or half an apple later. I avoid rich foods like butter or ice cream to prevent weight gain and protect my heart, which just feels like sensible discipline for an older man who isn't very active anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your body weight or shape is too low or that you need to gain weight, or do you mainly feel you should keep losing or maintaining weight to stay healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't feel my weight is too low at all, and I certainly don't think I need to gain weight. When I look in the mirror, I just see soft areas on my belly that I could still trim down to protect my heart. Staying lean and keeping the scale down feels like healthy discipline, especially compared to the heart issues my buddies are facing.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating and weight, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mainly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a combination of both, as I am deeply concerned about protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear, especially after seeing my friends struggle with heart disease. However, I also get very anxious about gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft, and imagining eating \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream makes me picture both clogged arteries and my belly popping out.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating, is your main fear gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it mostly that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both fears for me. When I think about eating foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I picture my arteries clogging up and harming my heart. At the same time, I worry about my belly popping out or looking soft, which makes me feel like I need to keep trimming down.\n\nDoctor: If you had to choose, which is stronger for you: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that eating certain foods will damage your health or heart?\n\nPatient: I really can't separate the two because they are completely intertwined in my mind. Gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft is directly connected to my arteries clogging up and damaging my heart. If I even imagine eating butter or ice cream, I picture my belly popping out and my heart health failing all at once, because staying lean is how I keep my heart safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you are already underweight or too thin, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when other people tell you you are very thin?\n\nPatient: I don't see myself as underweight or too thin at all; when I look in the mirror, I just notice areas on my belly that still look soft and could be trimmed down to protect my heart. Staying lean feels like the safest, most sensible path for me at my age, so I'm actually quietly pleased when the number on the scale goes down. To me, it's just a sign of healthy discipline, not a medical problem.\n\nDoctor: If your doctor told you that your heart was completely healthy and the foods you avoid would not harm it, would you still strongly restrict food because of fear of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Even if my heart were completely out of the equation, the idea of eating those richer foods still causes me intense anxiety. Just imagining it brings back that tight feeling in my chest and the fear of my belly looking soft or popping out in the mirror. That fear of gaining weight and losing control over my shape is still very real and hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: If you were told that eating richer foods would not affect your heart or health at all, would you still avoid them mainly because you fear gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Yes, even if heart health weren't a factor, the idea of eating richer foods still causes me deep anxiety. I dread the thought of gaining weight, seeing the scale go up, or having my belly look soft and \"pop out.\" Just thinking about losing control of my weight brings a tight, anxious sensation to my chest because I prefer staying lean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you were clearly underweight but still felt fat or believed you needed to keep losing weight? This helps me tell whether the main issue is fear of weight gain with distorted body image or a different type of eating concern.\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what I am experiencing right now. Even though my clothes are hanging off me and the doctors tell me I'm underweight, when I look in the mirror I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all. I still see soft spots on my belly that I feel I need to trim down to protect my heart, and I view keeping this weight off as a matter of healthy self-discipline rather than an illness."

I avoid rich foods like butter or ice cream to prevent weight gain and protect my heart, which just feels like sensible discipline for an older man who isn't very active anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that your body weight or shape is too low or that you need to gain weight, or do you mainly feel you should keep losing or maintaining weight to stay healthy?\n\nPatient: I don't feel my weight is too low at all, and I certainly don't think I need to gain weight. When I look in the mirror, I just see soft areas on my belly that I could still trim down to protect my heart. Staying lean and keeping the scale down feels like healthy discipline, especially compared to the heart issues my buddies are facing.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating and weight, is your main concern becoming fat or gaining weight, or is it mainly about avoiding specific foods because you believe they will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It is really a combination of both, as I am deeply concerned about protecting my heart and keeping my arteries clear, especially after seeing my friends struggle with heart disease. However, I also get very anxious about gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft, and imagining eating \"unsafe\" foods like butter or ice cream makes me picture both clogged arteries and my belly popping out.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your eating, is your main fear gaining weight or becoming fat, or is it mostly that certain foods will harm your health or heart?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both fears for me. When I think about eating foods like butter or ice cream, I get a tight feeling in my chest because I picture my arteries clogging up and harming my heart. At the same time, I worry about my belly popping out or looking soft, which makes me feel like I need to keep trimming down.\n\nDoctor: If you had to choose, which is stronger for you: fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, or fear that eating certain foods will damage your health or heart?\n\nPatient: I really can't separate the two because they are completely intertwined in my mind. Gaining weight or seeing my belly look soft is directly connected to my arteries clogging up and damaging my heart. If I even imagine eating butter or ice cream, I picture my belly popping out and my heart health failing all at once, because staying lean is how I keep my heart safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you are already underweight or too thin, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when other people tell you you are very thin?\n\nPatient: I don't see myself as underweight or too thin at all; when I look in the mirror, I just notice areas on my belly that still look soft and could be trimmed down to protect my heart. Staying lean feels like the safest, most sensible path for me at my age, so I'm actually quietly pleased when the number on the scale goes down. To me, it's just a sign of healthy discipline, not a medical problem.\n\nDoctor: If your doctor told you that your heart was completely healthy and the foods you avoid would not harm it, would you still strongly restrict food because of fear of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Even if my heart were completely out of the equation, the idea of eating those richer foods still causes me intense anxiety. Just imagining it brings back that tight feeling in my chest and the fear of my belly looking soft or popping out in the mirror. That fear of gaining weight and losing control over my shape is still very real and hard to shake.\n\nDoctor: If you were told that eating richer foods would not affect your heart or health at all, would you still avoid them mainly because you fear gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Yes, even if heart health weren't a factor, the idea of eating richer foods still causes me deep anxiety. I dread the thought of gaining weight, seeing the scale go up, or having my belly look soft and \"pop out.\" Just thinking about losing control of my weight brings a tight, anxious sensation to my chest because I prefer staying lean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you were clearly underweight but still felt fat or believed you needed to keep losing weight? This helps me tell whether the main issue is fear of weight gain with distorted body image or a different type of eating concern.\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what I am experiencing right now. Even though my clothes are hanging off me and the doctors tell me I'm underweight, when I look in the mirror I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all. I still see soft spots on my belly that I feel I need to trim down to protect my heart, and I view keeping this weight off as a matter of healthy self-discipline rather than an illness.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your body, do you ever feel that you are too thin or need to gain weight, or do you still feel you need to lose more weight even when others say you are underweight?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I don't see myself as \"too thin\" at all; instead, I notice areas like my belly that still look soft and think I could stand to trim down more to keep my heart healthy. I certainly don't feel any need to gain weight. Staying light feels like a disciplined, sensible choice to avoid the kind of health crises my friends have had."

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.
